## Página 4

01
Nouns/Articles/Quantifiers
NOUNS
There are different types of noun.
Concrete nouns
(things that can be touched, heard, seen, smelled or tasted)
fur,noise,tree,meal, drink, etc
Abstract nouns
(things that represent a quality, a state or an idea)
beauty,curiosity,ability,education,culture,e
Propernouns
(names usedfor specificthings, spelled withan initial capital letter)
Patrick,Paris,November,Jupiter,e
Nouns can be countable or uncountable.
Countable nouns
. They can be singular or plural.
a book > three books
onephoto>fiftyphotos
The plural form usually ends in -s, -es and -ies.
toy > toys
fox > foxes
watch > watches
baby > babies
Some plural forms are irregular. (See Appendix 4 on page 252
man >men
for more nouns with irregularforms.)
wife >wives
foot > feet
Uncountable nouns
They do not take a/an and have no plural form.
His advice was helpful.
He gave me lots of information.
They include the following:
-groupsmadeup of related items
furniture, clothing,equipment, fruit, rubbish,money
traffic, luggage, etc
- languages and fields of study
French,Spanish,Italian,history,biology,literature,etc
-sports,pastimes,leisure
baseball, cricket, football, etc
-substances and materials
water, butter,hydrogen, metal, petrol, etc
-actions/activities
jogging,reading,meditating, etc
- abstract nouns
death, news, advice, knowledge, proof, etc
-natural phenomena
lightning,light,rain,humidity,electricity,etc
·They can be itemised or'counted'with other nouns such as
a piece of advice, a bit of cheese, a slice of cake, a litre
piece,item,bit, slice, litre,jar,glass, etc.
ofwater, ajar ofjam, a glass of juice, etc
NOUNS ENDING IN -S
Some uncountable nouns end in -s and yet they take a
singular verb.
-illnesses:measles,diabetes,mumps
Mumps is an llness thatmakes the neck swollen and painful.
- sports and games: aerobics, gymnastics, billiards
Billiards is his favourite game and he's pretty good at it.
-areas ofstudy:physics,politics
Physics is a popular subject at this university.
-abstract ideas:thanks, news
Loneliness is a common problem for elderly people living on
-feelings: happiness, loneliness
their own.
Some nouns refer to one object having two parts and take a
These scissors are blunt. Have you got a sharper pair?
pluralverb:jeans,shorts,tights,trousers,glasses, scissors,
Those glasses look great on you!
scales, etc.
When a specific measurement is the subject of a sentence,
Three hours was a long time to wait.
it takes a singular verb even if the measurement is plural.
Two miles isn't toofar to walk.
Fifteen minutes is what it normally takes to cycle to work.
Some nouns are more common in the plural form and
The stairs were very steep and difficult for Grandad to climb.
take a plural verb: goods, remains, stairs, facilities,
Facilities for young people are excellent in this town.
refreshments,ruins,etc.
Refreshments are available during the interval.

## Página 5

Grammar
COMPOUNDNOUNS
Incompoundnouns,thefirstword classifiesthesecondword
a car park (= somewhere to park your car)
and is usually singular.
(a cars-park X)
a mountain climber (- someonewho climbs mountains)
(a mountains climber X)
Common combinationsare:
-noun+ noun
bedroom, water tank, credit card
-noun + verb
haircut, sky-diving,dog walker
-verb + noun
washing machine,driving licence, boxing ring
COLLECTIVE NOUNS
Collective nouns refer to a group of people, animals or objects.
We canusuallyusea singularorplural verbafter thesenouns
My family is coming to visit this summer.
depending on whetherwe are considering the noun asone entity or
(- They are travelling as one unit.)
asagroupof individual people,animalsorobjects.
My family are very creative.
(=Eachpersoninmyfamilyisverycreative.)
Some collectivenouns (police,folk, cattle) always takeaplural verb.
The police have arrested the gang leader.
Folk are complaining about his lack of talent
Adjectivesusedascollectivenounsalwaystakeapluralverb.
The elderly are more vulnerable than other citizens.
The famous often buy houses in this part of London.
ALERT!
Some nouns can be both countable and uncountable, but with a change in meaning. See Appendix 5 on
pages 253-254for examples.
There is a wood on the other side of the river.
This table is made of wood.
THE INDEFINITE ARTICLE (A/AN)
USE
EXAMPLE
with singular countablenouns
a burger, a uniform, an egg,an MP3 player
.with nouns that have not been mentioned before or are
Jill bought a book.
non-specific
I had an omelette for lunch.
with professions, nationalities, jobs
Chris is a lawyer.
Marie is an Italian.
I'm a chef.
to refer to one example in a larger group such as a
Ittakes manyyears ofstudytobecomeabarrister.
professionorspecies
An orca is part of the dolphin family.
with a proper noun when we are not familiar with the
Do you know a James Carter? He's sent me a birthday card.
personinquestion
There's a Marion at the door. She wants to speak to you.
with certain abstract nouns in a specific context: horror,
He has a hatred of spiders!
dislike,love,hatred,fear,knowledge,awareness,etc
She has a fear of heights.
with large numbers andfractions
This dress cost a thousand pounds!
The price had been reduced by a third.
with expressions ofquantity
There are a lot of people here today.
I'd like a kilo of grapes, please.
•for frequency and speed
I have my hair cut once a month.
The car was travelling at one hundred miles an hour.
ALERT!
A/an has a similar meaning to one. However, it does not always mean the same thing.
Terrific Tees has a black T-shirt in Large. (-The shop may have more than one; the exact number isn't important.)
Terrific Tees has one black T-shirt in Large. (= The shop only has one Tshirt in this size and colour combination.)

names of shops and companies

used as subjects, they take a singular or plural

verb depending on the noun they replace.

When neither and none are used with plural

nouns, they can take a singular or a plural verb.

Each refers to two or morepeopleorthings

whereas every refers to three or more. Every

cannot be used when there are only two people
(The judge listened carefully to every lawyer.X)

We can use any with a singular noun to mean 'it

doesn't matter which.We can also use some in
(-itdoesn'tmatterwhich book)
this way, but it usually has an impolite, couldn't

care less'kind of meaning.

(-I don't know the band and I don't careabout it)
We often use some to emphasise an
That's some outfit you're wearing! You look fantastic!
exclamation.
That's some house he lives in; it must have twenty rooms!
ALERT!
A few and a little mean 'some, a small number/amount of.However,few and little mean'not enough of.
There is a little time left. If you're quick, you'll get there before the shop closes.
There is little time left; I doubt our team will score a goal now.
There are a few places left on the excursion. If you book now, you should get one.
Few participants have signed up for the seminar. Do you think we should postpone it?
. We can use few and little with nouns that have a negative meaning (disabilities, problems, trouble, difficulties, etc).
In such cases, the overall meaning of the sentence becomes positive.
There was little trouble at the football match last Saturday. (-- There wasn't much trouble.)
There are few illnesses that modern medicine is unable to cure. (- There aren't many illnesses that modern medicine
can't cure.)

## Página 21

02
Prepositions
.Prepositions can be a single word or formed ofmore than one word.We useprepositions to describe the relationship
betweentwoormoreobjects.Theycanexpressthings suchastime,place,movement,reason,etc.
The coach will pick up the students at noon. (time)
Your lunch is on the table. (place)
Walk down the road and you'll see the bus stop. (movement)
She's offworkdueto illness. (reason)
.Someprepositions and adverbs arevery similar,but prepositions are mostoftenfollowed bya noun.Remember that
gerunds are also a kind of noun. Compare:
Jonaswalkspastthebakeryeverydayandbuysasandwich.(preposition+noun)
I was in the bakery when Jonas walked past. (adverb)
We canmodifyprepositionswith adverbs.
We'll dropby immediately after dinner.
I sit right behind Laura in maths.
Our train got in just before yours did.
In some structures, the preposition comes at the end of a clause or a sentence. This is most commonly seen in questions,
relative clauses and infinitive clauses.
Who are you talking to?
I don't know theman that she is sitting with.
My brother is a difficult person to buy for.
Aprepositional phrase isaprepositionwitha noun,apronounoranounphrase.
Thank you for my present.
These flowers are for you.
We're having dinner at a wonderful Indian restaurant.
Wecan alsofollowprepositions withawh-clause.Wedo not useathat clause.
Thank you for what you did.
Contrary to what many people think, she's actually quite a good singer.
ALERT!
.When to is used as a preposition, it can be followed by the gerund form.
I look forward to seeing you soon.
They're not used to getting up early.
PREPOSITIONS OFTIME
EXAMPLE
at
Our flight departs at three o'clock.
with time, special periods of time and in certain phrases
Are you going away at the weekend?
I'll be there at noon.
We always throw a party at New Year.
Don't forget to lock the front door at night.
in
Ionly work in the afternoon.
with parts of the day, months, seasons, years, centuries
George was born in September.
School starts again in autumn.
TheymovedtoFrancein2009.
King Henry Vill died in the sixteenth century.
on
My exams start on Monday.
with names of days, special days and dates
Let's go out for dinner on your birthday.
Tina and Pete are getting married on 21st April.
after,before, by, past
Sheila can't meet us until after nine o'clock.
with a certain point in time
Please beherebefore ourvisitorsarrive.
Please make sure you're home by eleven o'clock.
It was past midnight when I finally went to bed.

## Página 22

Unit 2
EXAMPLE
about,around
We left the house at about ten o'clock.
withatto be less specific
Let's visit Nicky in hospital at around six.
during, for, from... till, from... until, from ...to, in,
The children sometimes get bored during the summer holidays.
since, through, throughout, until,till
She's staying for three days.
with a point in time or a period of time
We'll be in Madrid from Monday to Friday.
Jonah passed his driving test in just a few weeks.
I haven't visited Johannesburg since 2012.
My husband coughed through the whole performance!
We don't have a free weekend until November.
ALERT!
We say in the morning, in the afternoon and in the evening, but at night and on Monday morning.
• We can use while to mean the same as during, but it is always followed by a verb clause.
He was talking on his mobile phone during the film.
He was talking on his mobile phone while the film was showing.
In American English, we say on the weekend rather than at the weekend.
InAmerican English,wecanomittheprepositionbeforethenamesofdays.
British English: I don't work on Wednesdays.
American English: I don't work Wednesdays.
British English: We're visiting friends from Friday to Monday
American English: We're visiting friends Friday through Monday.
There is a slight difference in meaning between in time and on time. In time means with enough time to spare'and
on time means 'exactly at the right time-not too late and not too early.Compare:
The city has excellent public transport and the buses are always on time.
Do you think we'll be in time for the bus?

EXAMPLE
at

us and namesof roads andsquares

on

to indicate two objects, one either side

We say on a chair and on a sofa, but in an armchair.
.We say at or on the corner of a street, but in the corner of a room.
We can use different prepositions depending on the meaning.
I'll meet you at the station. (= inside or outside)
I'll meet you in the station. (= inside)
We usually say in the sea, but we say at sea in the phrase lost at sea.
He's over one hundred years old!
You can't find boots like these for under f250.
She was driving at over 90 mph when she was stopped by the police.

## Página 24

Unit 2
ALERT!
. We often use up and down with the same meaning as along.
Drive up the road until you see the library. Then find somewhere to park.
Go down this corridor and you'll find her office on your right.
. We use on(to) and off for a bus, a coach, a train, a plane, a boat and a bike, whereas we use in(to) and out of for a
car or a taxi.
The passengers got on the plane and the cabin crew prepared for take-off.
Get into the taxi and tell him where we're going.
· We usually use in and on to express the position of something and into and onto to express the idea of movement
towards a place.
My swimming things are in the beach bag.
Please put my swimming things into the beach bag.
The actors were standing on the stage.
The actors ran onto the stage.
. We can use at and to after some verbs. At implies that the person isn't happy. Compare:
Pass the ball to Tom and he'll score a goal.
Don't throw things at your brother; it isn't very nice.
'Don't forget to lock the door',mum shouted to Pat.
Mum shouted at Pat for spilling coffee all over the table.
OTHERUSESOFPREPOSITIONS
EXAMPLE
because of, due to, from, out of,
The elderly lady died from pneumonia.
owing to
Owing to his lack of interpersonal skills, he wasn't given the job.
to show reason
against, beside, contrary to
The company made a profit this year against last year's loss.
to show contrast
Joseph really is quite tall beside his brother.
Contrary to popular belief, there are advantages to both parents working.
for
This program is for keeping track of expenses.
to show purpose
This fluid is for cleaning screens.
through
She succeeded in business through sheer determination.
to show how something happened
He recovered the use of his right arm through physiotherapy.
by, with
Ken always travelsbytrain as he doesn't drive.
to talk about what we use to do
I put the desk together with an electric screwdriver.
something
by
This is a novel by Jojo Moyes.
to introduce the person who did
I love thewater lily paintingsby Monet
something
between
Choose a number between one and ten.
within a range of numbers, dates,
There's quite a difference between entertainment today and in the 20th century.
ages
You can take holidaybetween 5th and 25th July.
They have five children between the ages of six and thirteen.
like
Sandy acts like my mother, yet she's only two years older than me!
to compare
despite,in spiteof
Despite thebad weather,the match went ahead.
toshowconcession
Inspiteoffeelingunwell,Judewenttowork.
Despite whateveryone else says,Ibelieve you,
as
As a teacher,Jeffis brilliant.
to show the role of someone or
As a container for flowers, this pot is no good; it's got a hole in the bottom.
something
besides
Besides Janet, Ialso spoke to Miriam and Jenny.
to meaninadditionto'
Have you done anything else today besides playing video games?
accordingto
According to the local paper, we're going to have a heat wave this weekend.
to reportwhatsomeonehassaid
Fifty people have lost their homes in the recent floods, according to the latest reports.

## Página 36

03
Word Formation/CommonlyConfusedVerbs
PREFIXES&SUFFIXES
. A prefix is a group of letters that is added to the beginning of an adjective, verb or noun in order to change the meaning,
often to make it negative. Common prefixes include:im-,in-,un-and dis-.
possible→impossible
credible → incredible
available→unavailable
agree →disagree
A suffix is also a group of letters, but it is added to the end of a word in order to change the meaning. Common suffixes
include: -ify, -ment,-ist and -ian.
sign → signify
enjoy → enjoyment
philanthropy→philanthropist
mathematics→mathematician
There are often other spelling changes, usually when a suffix is added to a word.
clear → clarify
long→length
vision→visual

## Página 37

Grammar
ALERT!
We often use im-before words beginning with m-and p-.
Theman stood immobileas thebig dog sniffed around his ankles.
I find it impossible to sleep if I've had a coffee before bed.
We often use in-and un-before words beginning with c-.
Your use of American spelling in this essay is inconsistent.
This chair is terribly uncomfortable - can I sit on the sofa?
We often use il- before words beginning with I-.
Spending petty cash on coffee and cakes is obviously an illegitimate use of company funds.
We often use ir-before words beginning with r-.
A lot of the information in your essay is irrelevant to the main topic.
COMMON SUFFIXESUSED TO CHANGE MEANING
SUFFIX
USE
EXAMPLES
-er, ee, -or, -ant,
to change a word describing an object, place or action to a
lawyer,employee,assessor,assistant,
-ian,-ist
noun describing a person related to it, or the name of a job
beautician,activist
-ese, -ian, -an, -ish
to describe nationalities and languages related to a country
Chinese,Italian,Mexican,Danish
-ian,-ician,-ist
to describe the person studying or related to a subject
physician,mathematician,biologist
-ism,-ist
-ism is used to denote a belief or movement and-ist the

person whofollows thatbelief ormovement

-ie,y
to make a familiar version of a word or a name

shampoo, etc),telly (=TM),wellies (=
Wellington boots)

## Página 38

Unit 3
ALERT!
When we use participle forms of verbs as adjectives, the present participle (-ing) has an active meaning and the
past participle (-ed) has a passive meaning.
Fireman took half an hour to reach the burning house and by then it was too late to save it. (= the house which
was burning)
This burnt toast is horrible: I think I'll have cereal instead. (-the toast which had been burnt)
Some suffixes do not change the part of speech.This is most common with nouns because we sometimes have, for
example, one noun for the person and another for the object, or one noun for the male and another for the female
child → childhoodneighbour →neighbourhood
relation →relationshipfriend →friendship
COMMONLYCONFUSEDVERBS
.There are a number of verbs in English which are sometimes confused because the meanings are very similar.There are
sometechniquesthat canhelpyouidentifywhichverbiscorrectinwhichcontext.
Some verbs may be mirror image verbs, ie theylook at the same action from two different perspectives.For example,
borrow and lend. Compare:
Jennyborrowed Lisa'sruler.
Lisa lent her ruler to Jenny.
.Other verbs are transitive or intransitive. A transitive verb is an action verb that needs a direct object. An intransitive
verb does not need a direct object. For example, raise and rise. Compare:
Mark knew the answer to the teacher's question and raised his hand eagerly. (Raise is transitive and we must follow it with
adirectobject-hishand.)
The sun rises early in the morning. (Rise is intransitive and no direct object is needed.)
.For other verbs, learning the difference in usage, meaning and structure will be helpful. For example, the verb may

reach:arrive in or at a place

Past Forms
PAST SIMPLE
verb -ed (or irregular form)
USE
EXAMPLE
.completed states or actions that happened
We received an award for the best Mediterranean cuisine in the city.

The chef chopped the tomatoes,sliced the onions and poured some olive
in the past
oil into the frying pan.

When we were in Canada, we often walked through the snow to school.
Expressions of Time
before yesterday, the other day,whenever, later,afterwards, before, back then, when, etc
ALERT!
Past Simple or Present Perfect?
The past simple is used when we refer to a completed action that happened at a specific time in the past, whereas
the present perfect is used when no specific time reference is made, or when an action is still relevant to the
present.
I visited the Colosseum last year.
I have visited the Colosseum.
When we use since, the past simple is often used in'the clause beginning with since and the present perfect is
used in the main clause.
I've grown to like Elena since I started working with her.
I've been meaning to call you since I got back from Spain.
Sinceshe stopped eatingprocessed foods, her healthhas really improved.
English, the same adverbs are often used with the past simple.
British English
American English
Have you already finished?
Did you already finish?
Julia's never stayed here beforeJulia never stayed here before.
PAST CONTINUOUS
was/were + verb -ing
USE
EXAMPLE
·actions in progress at a particular time in the past
We were driving along the coast yesterday evening.
an action in progress in the past that was interrupted by
While Tabitha was eating a cereal bar, her tooth came out!
anotheraction
· two actions in progress at the same time in the past
Andrew was jogging around the block while I was playing
basketball.
an action or situation used as an introduction to a more
I was listening to the radio when Mum told me your news.
significant event
temporary actions in the past
Last summer I was helping Dad out at the office.
annoying or amusing past habits (with always,

ALERT!
Past Simple orPast Perfect?
.In some sentences which talk about the past, it is clear which action happened first. In this case, we can use the
past simple for both actions. However, when the order of actions is not clear, or when we want to emphasise which
action happened first, we can use the past perfect simple or past perfect continuous for the first action.
I was disappointed becauseI missed the film.
My brother had eaten all the cake, so I didn't get a piece!
When one past action happens quickly after another past action, or when the second action is the result of the
first action, we must use the past simple for both actions.
Susantook her suitcaseoutof thecar and closed theboot.
James tripped on his son's toy and fell down the stairs.
Past Perfect Simple or Past Perfect Continuous?
If we want to emphasise the duration or the repetitive nature of something, we use the past perfect continuous.
I'd been telephoning all morning before they finally answered.
P'd telephoned about ten times before they finally answered.
OTHERFORMSUSEDTOREFERTOPASTTIME
FORM
USE
EXAMPLE
·used to +bare infinitive
past habits or states
Our loft apartment used to be a restaurant.
·would + bare infinitive
past habits, but not states
When we used to eat out as a family, Dad would always
order dessert foreveryone.

Gerunds&Infinitives
GERUNDS
FORM
Active
Passive
Present
(not) doing

(not) having been done
USE
EXAMPLES
As a noun, as follows:
aftercertainverbsand expressions
It's pointless trying tofind tickets for the film premiere.They're all sold out.
I miss notseeingCheryl everydaynowthat she's started her new job.
·after prepositions
We're keen on participating in the new experiment.
·as the subject or object of a verb
Smoking is detrimental to your health.
Sallyenjoys cookingand regularlymakes Sunday lunchforherfriends.
after theverbs go and come
Helen has gone shopping with her friends.
Do you want to come hoverboarding with us?
with'no'toexpressprohibition
No parking. This garage is in constant use.
This isa no smokingarea.

ALERT!
Dread can also be followed by a full infinitive in the phrase I dread to think .... We use it to show we are worried
about something.
I dread to think what she'll say when she finds out the truth.
Suggest can be followed by the following structures:
suggest + gerund
Alan suggested eating a more balanced diet.
suggest + (that) + object + (should +) bare infinitive
Alan suggested we eat a more balanced diet.
Alan suggested that we should eat a more balanced diet.
EXPRESSIONS FOLLOWED BY A GERUND

VERBS FOLLOWED BY A GERUND ORAFULL INFINITIVE
Someverbssuchasbegincan'tbearcontinueintend,loveandstartcanbefollowedbyagerundorafull infinitive
without a change in meaning.
I started learning French when I was stillat primary school.
I started to learn French when I was still at primary school.
However, there are others that can be followed by the gerund or the full infinitive, but there is a change in meaning.
VERB
EXAMPLE
•forget + gerund
forget an action in the past,usually in negative sentences
I'll never forget dropping my mobile phone in the bath!
forget + infinitive
=forget to complete something or a responsibility
We forgot to invite Gemma to our party.
'go on + gerund
-continue the same action
The oral exam had finished butthestudent went ontalking.
go on + infinitive
- do a different action after you have finished doing
The scientist walked on stage and went on to describe his
something else
experiment.
•mean + gerund
-have as a result, involve
Becoming a lawyer means studying very hard.
mean +infinitive
=intend,want
Dan didn't mean to insult his teacher.
need + gerund
-require
This light bulb needs replacing.
need +infinitive
passive meaning:need to be done
You really need to get your car fixed. It's a danger to you
and to other road users.
·regret + gerund
be sorry about a past action
I regret not taking a gap year after my A levels.
regret +infinitive
be sorry about making a negative announcement
I regretto informall passengers thatthethreeo'clocktrain
to Ely has been cancelled.
·remember + gerund
recall, not forget a past action
IrememberwatchingavideoaboutplacesonEarthyoucan
see clearly from space.
remember +infinitive
not forget to perform an action in the future
Hannah,remembertorecordthedocumentaryforme,please.
stop +gerund
not continue an action or habit
Can you stop talking, please? The film has started.
stop +infinitive
stop doing one action in order to do another one
Before you make your decision, stop to think about the
consequences.
try + gerund
do something for a while to find out if it works or if it is
If you have difficulty sleeping, why don't you try reading for
successful, enjoyable, etc
a while before turning out the light?
try + infinitive
attempt something to see if you can do it or not
We tried to explain the situation to her, but she just
wouldn't listen.

ALERT!
How could sb do sth ...?
We use this structure to say that we think somebody's behaviour is inappropriate, very surprising or wrong.
How could you tease that little girl?
How could Mark eat insects? That's just disgusting!
couldn't be + comparative
We use this structure to emphasise the extreme nature of something.
John couldn't be more different to his brother.
You're getting married! I couldn't be happier!
may / might as well
We use this phrase to make a suggestion when there is nothing more interesting, useful or better to do.
It doesn't look like anyone's going to turn up; we may as well go home.
There's nothing on TV tonight; we might as well watch a DVD.
How should...? / Why should ...?
We can use should in questions to indicate annoyance.
What time will Dan turn up?'How should I know? He never tells me anything!'
'Miriam's had an argument with Sophie!Why should I care?I don't like either of them.
SEMI-MODALS
semi-modal verb + bareinfinitive
There are a number of semi-modals. Unlike modal verbs, they can be used in a variety of tenses as well as in a gerund or
an infinitive form providing the meaning allows it.
USE
EXAMPLE

There is a difference between mustn't and don't have to. We use mustn't to say that something is prohibited
or forbidden. We use don't have to to say that something is optional; it is our choice whether we do it or not.
Compare:
Youmustn't takephotographs in this part of themuseum.It's not allowed.
You don't have to take photographs with your mobile phone; you can borrow my camera.
Wealso usedareinthesesetphrases:
Don't you dare tell our parents!
How dare you accuse me of lying!
I dare say Henry will forgive you eventually

We use this structure to show that we are not surprised by an action.
I might have known (that) Cassie would forget to bring any money with her!
needn't / shouldn't have + past participle
We can use this structure to thank someone politely for doing something.
What a beautiful bracelet! You really needn't have gone to so much trouble.
You shouldn't have sent me flowers, but thank you; they are beautiful!
There is a difference between didn't need to and needn't have. We use didn't need to to say that something was
unnecessary. We do not know if the action was done or not. We use needn't have to say something was unnecessary
eventhough itwas done.Compare:
There was plenty of food left over from the party, so I didn't need to cook anything. We made do with what we had.
There was plenty of food left over, so I didn't heed to cook anything. I made a vegetarian lasagna, though, because
it's Lucy's favourite.

H Read the article and circle the correct words.
Writing through the ages
Writing first appeared in about 3,2oo BC and was invented by the Sumerians of Mesopotamia. It consisted
of marks made with a reed on a clay (1) block /tablet-we call this earlyform of writing 'cuneiform.Most
of these (2) inscriptions / memoranda record details of commercial transactions. Other systems developed
independently in both China and central America.A writing system known as Linear Awas used in ancient
Greece, but its meaning remains a mystery as experts have not yet (3) encoded / deciphered it.
Themodern European alphabet is based on the Greek alphabet which itself derives from the
Phoenician (4) calligraphy / script. Adopted by the Romans and passed down to the Anglo-
Saxons this is the alphabet most widely used today - although today it has 26 (5) symbols /
characters rather than theoriginal 24.
The materials used for writing have also changed over the centuries. Ancient Egyptians
wrote on (6) papyrus / bamboo, which was made from a kind of plant fibre. In fact, the
word'paper' is derived from this. The (7) secretaries / scribes of ancient Greece and
Rome wrote on long (8) streamers / scrolls made from processed leather known
as (9) parchment /fleece. However, we must credit the ancient Chinese with the
invention of paper - a mix of plant fibres and rags which was mashed, smoothed
and dried. Its fabrication was a closely-guarded secret for hundreds of years.
In about 600 AD people first started using the (10) ballpoint / quill (a long
feather dipped in ink) to write with.

## Página 118

Unit 8
ALERT!
In defining relative clauses, the pronouns who, whom, that and which can be omitted when they are the object of the
verb in therelative clause.
The salesman who/that I consulted gave me sound advice about which mobile phone to purchase.
The salesman I consulted gave me sound advice about which mobile phone to purchase.
·When a relative pronoun is used with a preposition, the preposition can either come before the relative pronoun
(formal) or at the end of the clause (informal).Note that the words that and who cannot be used after a preposition.
We must usewhich and whom.
The customer care agent, to whom I spoke, advised me to exchange the item.
The customer care agent, who/whom I spoke to, advised me to exchange the item.
The coat for which Ipaid f145 is a lovely cream colour.
The coat that/whichIpaidf145for is a lovely cream colour.
When we refer to objects and we want to demonstrate possession, we usually use whose + noun. We can also use
noun + of which or that ...noun of.
I bought the picture from a tiny art gallery whose nameI can't remember.
I bought the picture from a tiny art gallery the name of which I can't remember.
I bought the picture from a tiny art gallery that I can't remember the name of.
The words whatever,whoever, whichever, wherever and whenevermay beused in defining relative clauses to give
the meaning of anything, anyone, anywhere, etc. They are used to talk about people or objects that are indefinite or
unknown.
You can't just park the car wherever you choose! (- in any place that you choose)
I gave Sally cash for her birthday so she can buy whichever handbag she wants. (-any handbag that she wants)
MODIFYINGRELATIVEPRONOUNS
In non-defining relative clauses, we can use expressions of quantity with of whom, of which and of whose to modify the
relativepronoun and make whatwe saymore specific.
half/one/two/three
The security company employsfifteen people, eight of whom used tobe police officers.
both/each
I boughtahugebarof chocolatefrom thelocal sweet shop,half of whichI ate onmy
either/neither
way home!
all/none
There werefour English teams playing in the Champion's leaguethis year,two of which
many/much
made ittothesemi-finals.
few/several
Agatha Christiewas an English crime novelist,most of whosebooks havebeen
some/any
adaptedfor television,radioand theatre.
thefirst/thelast
The word which may also be used in conjunction with connectors and prepositions in order to make something we say
make more specific
during which
We were invited to the opening ceremony of the new sportswear store in London,
despite which
during which a famous athlete gave a speech.
at which point
Falling sales have led to a drop in the share price, despite which the brand still retains
in which case
a high-profile public image.
inwhichevent
You have a rest until I finish tidying up, at which point we'll have a cup of tea.
REDUCEDRELATIVECLAUSES
Relative clauses can sometimes be reduced if they modify the subject of a sentence. The relative pronouns who, which or
that and the verb be can be removed.
reducing to an adjective
Anystudents who/thatare responsiblefor the damagewill begiven detention.
phrase
>Any students responsiblefor the damage will begiven detention.
This is the only convenience store which/that is located near our house.
>This is the only convenience store located near our house.
reducing to a prepositional
Members of staff who/that are on the shop floor must wear uniform.
phrase
>Members of staff on the shop floor must wear uniform.
Shops which/that are on this list stock environmentally-friendly products.
>Shops on this list stock environmentally-friendly products.
118

## Página 119

Grammar
PARTICIPLE CLAUSES
We can use participles in participle clauses to make sentences shorter.
In general, we use a present participle (verb + -ing) when the meaning is active and a past participle (verb + -ed or
irregular form) when the meaning is passive.
A perfect participle (having (been) + past participle) is used to emphasise that one action finishes before the other.
Participle clauses can be positive or negative.
Knowing she needed a new dishwasher, Janice spent the afternoon online looking at what was available.
Purchased online, the dishwasher was delivered the following week.
Not having used a dishwasher before,Janice read the instructions a few times.
USE
EXAMPLE
·to replace the relative
The restaurant which is opening at the weekend specialises in Mexican cuisine.
pronoun and the verb
> The restaurant opening at the weekend specialises in Mexican cuisine.
The woman who has been hired to restructure the company is American.
>Thewomanhired torestructure thecompanyisAmerican.
·with conjunctions and
While I was shoppingfor a birthday present,Ibumped into an old school friend.
prepositions
>While shopping for a birthday present, I bumped into an old school friend.
Jerry put the chicken in the oven and then he started to prepare the vegetables.
>After putting the chicken in the oven, Jerry started to prepare the vegetables.
to express time
When we arrived at the airport, we saw that our flight was delayed.
> Arriving at the airport, we saw that our flight was delayed.
AfterI chose a present for Dad's 6oth birthday,I had to find a suitable card.
>Having chosen a present for Dad's 6oth birthday,Ihad to find a suitable card.
·to express condition
If they are walked twiceaday,dogs areless likelyto misbehave in thehouse.
> Walked twice a day, dogs are less likely to misbehave in the house.
·toexpressreason
I needed a translatorduring the conferencebecauseI couldn't speak Spanish.
>Not being able to speak Spanish,Ineeded a translator during the conference.
·toexpressresult
There had been heavy rain all night and as a result flooding in many areas was caused.
>There had been heavy rain all night, causing flooding in many areas.
ALERT!
.The subject of the participle clause and the main clause is usually the same.
Danielpacked hisbags and thenheleft the apartment.
>Having packed his bags, Daniel left the apartment.
If the subject of the participle clause and the main clause is not the same, then the participle will have its own subject.
After the dishes were washed, Zara lay on the sofa and read her book.
> Dishes washed, Zara lay on the sofa and started reading her book.

## Página 130

09
Conditionals
CONDITIONAL SENTENCES
Conditional sentences express orask aboutfactual implications,orhypotheticalsituations andtheir consequences.Complete
conditional sentences contain a conditional clause (the if clause) and a main clause.When the conditional clause comes first, it
is separated from the main clause by a comma. We do not use a comma when the conditional clause follows the main clause.
If we set off now, we'll arrive on time.
We'll arrive on time if we set off now.
ZERO CONDITIONAL if/when +present > present
USE
EXAMPLE
.facts,general truths
If you press this button on theTV remote control, the volume increases.
to say what always happens in a particular
When he eats peanuts, he has an allergic reaction.
situation
ALERT!
.We can also use a modal verb in zero conditional sentences.
If he can buy himself new clothes every weekend, he can afford to get me a birthday present.
• We use an imperative in the main clause when we give an instruction.
When the fire alarm sounds, leave the building in an orderly fashion.
FIRSTCONDITIONALif +present >will/modalverb +bare infinitive
USE
EXAMPLE
torefer toa probableor possiblefuture result
Jamie won't get the part if hedoesn't speak Italian.
She'll feel uncomfortable if she isn't wearing the right clothes.
·offers
If I see Helen, I'll invite her to the party.
suggestions
If you're leaving now, you can give me a lift.
warningsoradvice
Fiona may be upset if you're late.
ALERT!
We can add the word then to emphasise that the result of an action depends on the condition in the first part of the
sentence beingfulfilled.
If you can't help with the costumes, then I'lljust have to do it myself.
.We can also use be going to in the main clause of first conditional sentences.
She's going to be upset if she doesn't get the part.
When we give commands with the first conditional, we use an imperative in the main clause.
if you need a lift to the theatre, call me.
.We can use a future tense in the conditional clause when it expresses willingness or refusal to do something, or
insistence on something.
If Betty will book the tickets, I'll collect them from the box office.
If Tom won't tell me what Janet said, I'll ask her myself!
If you will tease the cat, it's not surprising that she scratches you!
We can use would or will with a bare infinitive in the conditional clause to make polite or formal requests.
If you would like to follow me, I'll take you to your room.
If you will show me your tickets, I can lead you to your seats.
SECONDCONDITIONALif + past > would/modalverb + bare infinitive
USE
EXAMPLE
unlikely situations in the future
If Iris earned more money, she could buy a bigger house.
·hypothetical situations
If Lorna spoke Russian, she would apply for theposition
advice (usually with If I were you)
If I were you, I'd avoid watching that new horror film!
·polite requests
It would belovely, if you wereable to join us.
·hopes or regrets
If they lived closer to each other, they would see each other more often.
·habitual actions in the past
If Jenny went out at night, she would always take a taxi home.
130

## Página 131

Grammar
ALERT!
We can use were instead of was in the conditional clause. This is more common in a formal style.
If I were a better dancer, I would audition for the show.
If the song were more catchy, maybe more people would like it.
THIRD CONDITIONAL if + past perfect > would have/modal perfect + past participle
USE
EXAMPLE
past states oractions thatwere
If I hadn't been waiting for you in the rain, I wouldn't have caught that horrible cold!
possible, but didn't happen
If the tickets had been reasonably priced, John might have suggested that we take the
whole family.
· past satisfaction or regret
If you hadn't helped me with my project, we wouldn't have become such good friends.
Sam could have won thedance competition if he hadn't taken that nasty fall.
INVERSIONINCONDITIONALS
were (to) or had.
FORM
EXAMPLE
First conditional
Should you require any help,please don't hesitate to contact us.
Should ... bare infinitive
(=If yourequireanyhelp,pleasedon'thesitateto contactus.)
Second conditional
Should your details on the form be wrong,your application would be rejected.
Should... bare infinitive
(- If your details on the form were wrong, your application would be rejected.)
Were ... full infinitive
Were you to be selected for the position, you would need to undergo a month's training.
(-If you were selected for the position, you would need to undergo a month's training.)
Third conditional
Had weknown how poor thereviews were going to be, wewouldn't have booked tickets
Had... past participle
to see the opera.
(- If we had known how poor the reviews were going to be, we wouldn't have booked
tickets to see the opera.)
ALERT!
We often use a negative form with inversion in the third conditional: Had ... not.
Had they not found the fire exit, they might have been trapped in the burning building.
(- If they hadn't found the fire exit, they would have been trapped in the burning building.)
MIXED CONDITIONALS
a variety of situations.
FORM
USE
EXAMPLE
If +pastsimple>
the real future result
If she came home late last night, she will be feeling tired today.
will/can/may/might +
of arealpast event or
(= She did come home late last night, so she will be feeling tired
bare infinitive
situation
today.)
If + past tense >
•the real present or past
If you lent him money, you've made a terrible mistake.
present perfect
result of a real past event
(=You did lend himmoney,but you have madea terrible
or situation
mistake.)
If + past perfect >
.hypothetical present result
If Julie hadn't stopped to help the injured dog,shewouldn't be its
wouldlcould/might+
ofahypotheticalpast
proud ownernow.
bare infinitive
event or situation
(- Julie did stop to help the injured dog, so she is its proud
owner now.)
If + past tense>
hypotheticalpastresult
If Elizabeth were an honest person, her boss might not have fired
would/could/might
ofahypotheticalpresent
her.

event or situation

CONDITIONALS WITHOUT IF
FORM

ALERT!
Although we normally use unless with a verb in the affirmative, we can also use a verb in the negative in certain cases.
She'll go to the University of Kent, unless her grades aren't good enough.
'lf they offer you the job, will you take it?'Not unless the salary is better than the one I have now'
Unless does not always mean if...not. When the negative condition after if goes against known facts, then unless
cannot be used.
You'd be happier if you didn't live alone.
(Known fact = You live alone. We can't say: You'd be happier unless you lived alone.)
happen. It does not mean the same as if. Compare:
I'll take a map in case I get lost.
(-I don't know if I'll get lost or not, but I'll take map anyway.)
I'll ring you if I get lost.
(= I will only ring you if I get lost.)
IMAGINARYCONDITIONS
We can use imagine,say,suppose/supposing (that) and what if to talk about imaginary conditions.There are several
structures. They are often used without a main clause.

USE

## Página 149

10
Wishes/Regrets/OtherStructures
WISH &IF ONLY
.We can use wish to talk about how we feel and about how other peoplefeel. However, we can only use if only to talk
about how we feel. Compare:
I wish Joel wasn't working this weekend. (howIfeel)
Joel wishes he wasn't working this weekend. (how Joel feels)
If only Joel wasn't working this weekend. (how Ifeel; we don't know how Joel feels)
Wish and if only are very similar in meaning, but if only can be more emphatic, Compare:
If only I'd worked harder. Now I'll have to retake my exams.
I wish I'd worked harder. I'd have got a better mark.
. As in second conditional sentences, we can also use were for all persons after wish and if only.
if only I were better at tennis.
I wish he were more interested in recycling things.
FORM
USE
EXAMPLE
wish/if only+
to say that something is not

(= It is very polluted.)

(- She isn't free.)
to express ourdissatisfaction

withafutureplan
(=You aren't coming with us and I'm not happy about that.)
wish/if only +
to express regrets about a

past perfect

(-The weather wasn't warm and sunny.)
tense

(Wedidn'ttakewarm clotheswith us.)

(= He does make fun of me: desire for him to change his behaviour)

ALERT!
We cannot use would to talk about a change to the present or future that is impossible, or a change to the past. For
these situations, we have to use a past tense for the present or future, and a past perfect tense for the past.
[Sandra wishes one of her six children would be a girl X)
Sandra wishes one of her six children was a girl.
(If-only we would have known about the damage to the environment earlier. X)
If only we had known about the damage to the environment earlier.
Be careful not to confuse hope with wish. Although hope has a similar meaning to wish, wish refers to something that
I wish the library was open today. (= The library is closed today.)
I hope the library is open today.
(=It's possible that the library is open today-that's what I want.)
We can also use hope followed by the past simple as an expression of goodwill.
We hope you enjoyed your stay at our farmhouse.

ALERT!
We can also use would rather/sooner to refuse permission or make suggestions politely.
I'd rather you didn't go out this Saturday. Grandma's coming to see us.
(- You can't go out this Saturday because Grandma's coming to see us.)
I don't feel like going out tonight. I'd sooner watch a good film on TV.
(- Let's watch a good film on TV instead of going out.)
We can also use would prefer in set expressions. We use I would prefer it if + subject + past tense to ask someone
politely not to do something in the present or the future. We use I would have preferred it if + subject + past perfect
to express our dissatisfaction with something that happened in the past.
I would prefer it if you didn't play your music that loudly.
( Please stop playing your music that loudly.)
I would have preferred it if you hadn't spent so much money.
(-I'm not happy about you having spent so muchmoney.)

## Página 163

11
ADJECTIVESTADVERBS
ADJECTIVES
.Adjectives describe nouns, giving us more information about them.Most adjectives are used before a noun (attributive
position) or after a linking verb such as appear, be, become, feel, get, look, sound, taste (predicative position).
It was a relaxing holiday.
The holiday was relaxing.
.Some adjectives describe what kind of thing something is. They are known as classifying adjectives. For example, main,
entire, chemical,medical, only, indoor, outdoor, principal, etc.
The nurse took my medical history.
(My history that the nurse took was medical. X)
.Other adjectives give emphasise and are known as emphasising adjectives.For example, mere, sheer, utter.
Thebedroomwasinutterchaos.
(The chaos the bedroom was in was utter: X)
TYPE
POSITION
EXAMPLE
.before a noun (attributive)
most adjectives
We stayed in a wonderful hotel.
aftera linkingverb (predicative)
The hotel we stayed in was wonderful.
classifyingadjectives
onlybeforeanoun
We sawthe film at an outdoor cinema.
(Thecinemawhere-wesawthefilmwas outdoor:X)
emphasising adjectives
onlybeforeanoun
Itwassheerluckthatbroughtustothislocation.
(Theluckthatbroughtustothislocation-was-sheer:X)
ALERT!
Adjectives always come after indefinite pronouns such as something, anyone, everything, etc.
I'm looking for something special.
(I'm looking for special something:X)
We can use some adjectives that describe a particular group as nouns. We usually use the word the and a plural
verb with these adjectives.
Richpeopleareoftenseeninthisrestaurant.
The rich are often seen in this restaurant.
·Certain adjectives beginning with the letter a (asleep, afraid, alive, ashamed, alone, awake) are not usually used
before a noun. This is also the case with adjectives that are used to describe health and feelings.
Be quiet or you'll wake the children; they're asleep.
(Be quiet or you'll wake the asleep children. X)
FORMINGADJECTIVES
There is no general rule for forming adjectives.
Adjectives formed from other words
commonsuffixesused:-ablel-ible,-al/-ial,-ant/-
access > accessible
tragedy> tragic
ent, -ative,-ous/-eous/-ious,-ful,-ic/-ical,-ish,
nutrition > nutritional
fool > foolish
-ive, -less, -y.
confidence>confident
impress>impressive
prevent>preventative
help>helpless
danger>dangerous
bend > bendy
plenty>plentiful
Present and past participles
· We often use present (ing) and past (-ed)
interesting,frightening,amusing,pleasing
participles as adjectives to describe feelings.
interested,frightened,amused,pleased
· Present participles describe a feeling that
The new manager is intimidating, isn't he?
something causes (active meaning).
(-He intimidates me.)
The film was rather boring.
(- It bored us.)
· Past participles describe a feeling that someone
I felt intimidated when I met the new manager.
experiences (passive meaning).
(-Iwas intimidated byhim.)
Wefeltboredwhilewatchingthefilm.
(-Weexperiencedboredom.)

## Página 164

Unit 11
Compoundadjectives
We can combine words tomake compound
adjectives.They usually have a hyphen when
used beforea noun.Common combinations are:
-adjective + noun
full-time student,low-costaccommodation
-noun + adjective
duty-free goods,lifelong friend
-verb +preposition/adverb
drive-incinema,knock-down prices
-self +verb, adjective,noun
self-cleaning oven,self-employed plumber,self-service restaurant
.We often add a present or past participle to an
James Bond is a well-known character.
adverb, an adjective or a noun.
You'll need a pair of hard-wearing boots.
The recipe calls for sun-dried tomatoes.
When a noun is used to make a compound
a two-week holiday (a two-weeks holiday X)
adjective, it is usually singular.
a five-hour flight (a five-hours flight X)
a ten-year-old boy (a ten-years-old-boy X)
ADJECTIVEORDER
When we use more than one adjective to describe a noun, we generally put opinion adjectives before factual adjectives.
We stayed in a beautiful four-star hotel.
(We stayed in a four-star beautiful hotel: X)
If there are other adjectives, we put them in a set order as follows:
opinion
size
age
shape
colour
origin
material
noun
stunning
new
oval
wooden
table
interesting
blackandwhite
French
film
fantastic
little
black
leather
wallet
ALERT!
.It sounds clumsy to put more than three adjectives before a noun. If we want to give lots of information about
something,we use additional clauses.
[We stayed in a luxurious, newly-renovated, five-star, London hotel.]
We stayed in London in a luxurious, five-star hotel which had been newly renovated.
.When we use two adjectives to describe different parts of the same thing, we put the word and between them.
We had a pink and white en suite bathroom.
(-Some parts of thebathroomwere pink and other parts were white.)
Sometimes two adjectives can describe contrasting aspects of the same thing; it seems surprising that they go
together. In such situations, we often use the words but or yet between them.
It was a slow-paced yet exciting film.
She spent a lot of time in her large but well-kept garden.
GRADABLE&UNGRADABLEADJECTIVES
Adjectives can be gradable or ungradable depending on their meaning. Gradable adjectives are those that describe a point
on a scale. For example, the adjectives warm and cold are points on the how hot something is'scale.Therefore, they are
gradable adjectives. Most adjectives are gradable. Ungradable adjectives are those that describe the extremes of a scale.
For example, the adjectivesboiling and freezing are the extremes of the‘how hot something is'scale.
Gradable adjectives
can be strengthened by using words such as really,very,
The weather is extremely cold in Helsinki at this time of year.
extremely,most (formal),pretty (informal),rather,so,terribly.
The guided tour was pretty good.
The climate in Egypt is terribly dry.
can be weakened by using words such as slightly,fairly, a
This hotel is a bit expensive, I'm afraid.
bit, a little bit, somewhat (formal).
The manager was somewhat unfriendly.
Ungradable adjectives
cannot be strengthened or weakened, but the meaning of
Our holiday in Dubai was absolutely brilliant!
the adjective can be emphasised by using words such as
This is utterly ridiculous!
absolutely,utterly,completely and totally.
The illness left him completely deaf in his right ear.
can be used with words such as almost, nearly,practically
My mobile phone is almost dead; where can I charge it?
and virtuallyto indicate a point closeto the extremeof the
His handwriting is so bad that it's practically illegible!
scale described by the adjective.
Thispuzzleisvirtuallyimpossible.

## Página 165

Grammar
ALERT!
. The modifier quite can be used with gradable and ungradable adjectives. However, the meaning changes
depending on the adjective it modifies and the stress and intonation.
Thefood was quite tasty. (gradable, no stress =fairly tasty)
The food was quite tasty. (gradable, stress on adjective - tastier than expected)
The food was quite tasty. (gradable, stress on adverb not as tasty as expected)
The food was quite delicious. (ungradable, stress on adverb = completely delicious)
ADVERBS
USE
EXAMPLE
Adverbs modify or give extra information about verbs,
Did you arrive safely?
adjectives, otherwords orwholeclauses.
The waters in the Caribbean are incredibly clear.
Our team was easily defeated.
Unfortunately,we had to cancel our trip to Barbados.
Someadverbssuchasreally,almost,quite,pretty,fairly,etc
The music was playing really loudly!
canmodifyanotheradverb.
Lucy didpretty well in her final exams.
Adverbs can also give information about
- time.
He arrived at our house early on Saturday morning.
- manner.
He arrived at our house early on Saturday morning.
- place.
He arrived at our house early on Saturday morning.
Adverbs can also show
frequency.
I seldom get to just sit and relax.
Foreign travel is often something we take for granted.
We've rarely met such interesting people.
- degree.
The travel agency was extremely busy when I popped in.
Backpacking is fairly cheap.
FORMINGADVERBS
Adverbs have a strong connection with adjectives. They are often formed by adding -ly to the adjective.
Adverbs ending in -ly
These are usually adjectives ofmanner or
degree.
-adjective +-ly
calm > calmly, fair > fairly
- adjective ending in -y, -y becomes -i then
easy>easily,happy>happily
add -ly
- adjective ending in -l, add -ly
careful > carefully,beautiful >beautifully
-adjective ending in -e, add -ly
fortunate>fortunately,extreme>extremely
-adjective ending in -ic, add-ally
tragic >tragically,basic>basically
Adverbs ending in -ward(s) or -wise
• These endings are usually added to nouns
after>afterward/afterwards
orprepositions.
back>backward/backwards
to>toward/towards
price>pricewise
length>lengthwise
health>healthwise
Adverbs with the same form as adjectives
·For example, back, close, daily, early,
The charter flight arrived early.
enough, far, fast, fine, ill, kindly, left, little,
We caught an early flight.
long,low,pretty, short, still, straight, wel,
Stay close to me so you don't get lost.
wide,wrong
We can walk there; it's quite close.
'Open wide,' said the dentist.
It's a wide road, so there's room to park.

## Página 166

Unit 11
Adverbs with two forms
Some adverbs have a base form and an -ly
form with differentmeanings.
close (- near, not far away)
She lives very close to the office.
closely (-carefully, or in a way that
Your financial situation will be examined closely before the loan is approved.
involves sharing ideas and information with
The team is successfulbecause all members work together closely.
one another)
deep (to a great depth)
We walked deep into the forest and found somewhere to put up our tent.
deeply (to a great extent)
I deeply regret what I said to you last night. I'm sorry.
direct (= without stopping)
My wife and I flew direct to New York.
directly(-immediately,or involving only
I was sotired that I wentdirectlytomyroom,
the two people or things mentioned)
I hold you directly responsible for this accident.
free (without paying)
Her parents own the theatre, so she gets in free.
freely (without restrictions)
Here's a key to your room so you can come and go freely.
hard (with great force/effort, or carefully)
He hit the ball hard and ran.
She spoke so softly that I had to listen hard in order to hear her.
hardly (scarcely, almost not)
I hardly know her; I only met her once.
high (to a great height)
Can you throw a ball high into the air?
highly (to a great extent)
He's a highly respected doctor.
late (not early or on time)
He often pays his bills late.
lately (recently)
Have you seen Jenna lately?
right (immediately,or showing direction)
I liked him right from the start.
Turn right at the bottom of this road.
rightly (with good reason)
It's worrying news and you are rightly concerned about what it means for you.
ALERT!
Remember that there are a few adjectives that look like adverbs. For example, costly, friendly, lonely, lovely, ugly,
etc. We cannot make these words into adverbs in the usual way. Sometimes we can use an expression.
(He spoke friendlily to the students. X)
He spoke to the students in a friendly way/manner.
COMPARATIVE&SUPERLATIVEADJECTIVES
.Weuse comparativeadjectives to compare two ormorepeople,animals or things.
A holiday in the Caribbean is likely to be more expensive than a holiday in Spain.
.Weuse superlativeadjectives to distinguish one or morepeople,animals or things froma number ofother people,
animals or things.
The Caribbean holiday was the most expensive of the ones I looked at.

## Página 167

Grammar
ALERT!
When we form the comparative and superlative forms of one-syllable adjectives ending in -ed, we do not add -er or
-est.Instead,weuse the words moreand most.
Tina was more tiredthan usual afterthisflight.
(Tina was tireder than usual after this flight. X)
Most one-syllable adjectives can also form the comparative and superlative with more and most, especially when
we want to emphasise something.
Sam is more late than he's ever been before. I hope everything's OK.
This is the piece of work you should be most proud of. It's brilliant!
When superlative adjectives are used, the can be replaced by a possessive.
Have you read her latest novel?
Superlative adjectives can be modified by numbers.
Is Bologna the second most affluent city in Italy?
Wecanusecompoundadjectivesin comparativeandsuperlativeforms
Simon is better-lookingthanhis brother.
Julia is the most hard-working young lady I've met.
COMPARATIVE&SUPERLATIVEADVERBS
.Weuse comparativeadverbsto comparetwo or moreactions.
Karen travels on business more regularly than her husband does.
.We use superlative adjectives to show the highest degree of one or more actions within a group of actions.
Benspokethemostengaginglyabouthisexperience.

ALERT!
We use the word than to introduce a clause or a noun after comparative adjectives or adverbs.
London is larger than Athens
Steve speaks Chinese more fluently than I thought he would.
After superlative adjectives and adverbs, we can use the word in before singular nouns and the word of before
plural nouns.
Charlieis thetallest childintheclass.
Duncan is the youngest of her children.
INTENSIFYINGORWEAKENINGCOMPARATIVES&SUPERLATIVES

byfareasily

ALERT!
When we want to contrast two related qualities, we always use more, not -er. We can also use not so much ... as and
rather than.
Kelly is more relieved than disappointed that her business trip was cancelled.

Kelly is relieved rather than disappointed that her business trip was cancelled.

Holiday travel tips
Going on holiday by plane? Check out our super travel tips!
· It's a good idea to have a (1) checklist / check-in with all your travel requirements written on it. Some websites
offer ready-to-use ones which you can print off.
• Pack all your essential documents into a (2) carry-on / self-catering bag which you can take onto the plane
with you. (Don't forget that you can only take one piece of (3) lost / hand luggage with you onto the plane.)
. This cabin bag should contain your passport, tickets, (4) foreign currency / exchange rate (if you're going
abroad), cash cards and your (5) itinerary / excursion, so you know roughly what you'll be doing every day. You
should also pack essential medical supplies into this bag.
· On most airlines you can take a suitcase weighing up to 20 kg - try and buy a light-weight model with wheels
and a (6) backpack / padlock for extra security. Make sure it has a (7) luggage label / boarding pass which
clearly states your name, address and (8) cancellation / destination. A brightly-coloured ribbon, tied onto your
case, will also help you to identify it quickly and easily on the (9) baggage carousel / package holiday.
Enjoy your holiday!

## Página 181

12
Reported Speech/The Subjunctive
REPORTEDSPEECH
When we report what someone said, we can use direct speech with quotation marks or we can use reported speech. In
reported speech the tenses, word-order and pronouns are often different from those in the original sentence.
Iwork near your office,'she said.
> She said (that) she worked near my office.
REPORTED STATEMENTS:TENSECHANGES
.Themost common reporting verbs for statements are say and tell.Tell is followed by an object.When speaking we often
omit the word that.
.When wereport commands or requests,wecan usetellor ask with a full infinitive.
Direct Speech
Reported Speech
Present Simple/Continuous
PastSimple/Continuous
Icommutetoworkeveryday,Jakesaid.
Jake said (that) he commuted to work every day.
amcompletingthequestionnaire,TamarasaidtoMrsBanks.
Tamara told MrsBanks (that) shewas completing the
questionnaire.
Present Perfect Simple/Continuous
Past Perfect Simple/Continuous
Veronica has worked in the field ofmedicine for fifteen years,
Matt said (that) Veronica had worked in the field of
Matt said.
medicine forfifteen years.
We've been living in the cityfor three years, Jenny told us.
Jennytold us (that) they had been living in the cityfor
three years.
Past Simple/Continuous
Past Perfect Simple/Continuous
"t snowed heavily,Mariesaid
Marie said (that) it had snowed heavily.
"We've been thinking about moving house, my grandparents
My grandparents told us (that) they had been thinking
told us.
about moving house.
Future Simple
Would + bare infinitive
'llreconnect the printer' Marcus said
Marcus said (that) he would reconnect the printer.
Future Continuous
Would + be + verb -ing
Jenny will be working on Saturday, Tim said to me.
Tim toldme (that) Jennywould be working on Saturday.
Be going to
Was/Weregoingto
We're going to be late Jeff told us.
Jeff told us (that) we were going to be late.
Imperative
Full infinitive
‘Take your time, my boss said to me.
My boss told me to take my time.
'Don't waste time online, please, Mum said.
Mum asked us not to waste time online.
Modal / Semi-modal
Pastformofmodal
George can help you out with the photocopying; Peter said.
Peter said (that) George could help out with the
photocopying
We must arrive by noon, Wendy said.
Wendy said (that) we had to arrive by noon.
I'm not able to speak French; I told him.
I told him (that) I wasn't able to speak French.
You may be offered the job, she said.
She said (that) I might be offered the job.
First Conditional
Second Conditional
'f you take my advice, you'll be fine, Milly said.
Milly told me (that) ifI took her advice, I would be fine.

REPORTEDQUESTIONS
The same tense changes that occur when we report statements also occur when we report questions.
The word order also changes.We do not use the question form;weuse the statement form.
The most common reporting verbs for questions are ask and want to know.We can also use enquire in formal situations
and wonder when we are asking ourselves something.
FORM
DIRECTSPEECH> REPORTEDSPEECH
With a question word
question word + subject + verb

> I wondered how he did that.
Without a question word
if/whether + subject + verb

>Theassistantwantedtoknowwhetherweneededanyinformation.
'Are you feeling unwell?'asked the manager.
> The manager enquired whether I was feeling unwell.

REPORTINGWITHNOUNS
In addition to reporting verbs,we can also use a noun followed by a clause to report what someone says to give more
information.
The manager announced that all staff would receive a f5,ooo bonus and everyone felt very excited.
>The manager's announcementthat all staff would receive a f5,ooo bonuswas met with great excitement.

promise,remark,response,recommendation,

THESUBJUNCTIVE
USE
The subjunctive form is the bare infinitive form of a verb. It can be used to refer to the past, the present and the future.
something
It is vital that Jamie communicate withhead office.
We can use a passive or negative form of the subjunctive.
Theproposal that the opera housebedemolishedwas rejected.
I recommend that younot leave your personal belongings in theoffice overnight.
Aftercertainverbs
advise, ask, command, demand, desire, insist, order,
Clara advised that he bepart of her newteam.
propose, recommend, require, request, suggest, urge
The boss insisted that Tom be at work by eight o'clock every
morning.
After certain adjectives
advisable,best,certain, crucial, desirable, eager,
It is advisable that every employee take out a pension plan.
essential,imperative,important,necessary,preferable,
It is essentialthatboth parties be aware of theterms of the
recommended, urgent,vital,willing
contract.
After certain nouns
idea, order, proposal, recommendation, request,
Our proposal states that the staff canteen serve hot meals
suggestion
between 12pmand 2pm.
Her final request was that her son take over the company.
In certainfixed expressions
be that as it may (shows that a decision stays the same
'lt wasn'tmyfault!'"Be that as it may,I'm still askingforyour
even ifsthistrue)
resignation'
come what may (=despite anything that might happen)
Come what may, we have to reduce our prices.
if need be (-if necessary)
If need be, we'll hire more staff.
(then) sobe it (shows that sb accepts sth despitebeing
If we need to cut back on spending in order to save the
unhappy about it)
company,(then)sobeit.
ALERT!
Although the use of the subjunctive is more common and not so formal in American English, it is seen as very
formal in British English.British English speakers often prefer to usealternatives.
subjunctive:Itis advisablethathebook a roomfortheseminar.
should + infinitive: It is advisable that he should book a room for the seminar.
present simple: It is advisable that he books a room for the seminar.
for + subject + full infinitive:It is advisable for him to book a room for the seminar.

## Página 203

13
ThePassive/TheCausative
THEPASSIVE
USE
We use the passive:
when the agent (the person or thing that does the action) is unknown.
The office has been painted. Someone must have done it over the weekend.
to emphasise the action and not the agent.
Theconferenceisbeingheld inParisthis year.
when it isnotnecessarytostatetheagentbecauseit isobvious.
The young man was arrested for dangerous driving.
whenwewanttobelessdirectandmorepolite.Compare:
Will youmarkouressaysbeforethenextlesson? (direct;lesspolite)
Will our essays be marked before the next lesson? (indirect; more polite)
when wewantto avoid blaming someonefor something,orto avoid personal responsibility.
I can see therubbishhasn'tbeen takenout.
Oh dear! This glass hasbeenbroken.
The passive is frequently used:
in formal written English.
The outstanding balance must be paid within 30 days.
.for rules and procedures.
Lights must be turned off when leaving the room.
·todescribeprocesses.
Light energy is absorbedby chlorophyll in the chloroplasts.

## Página 204

Unit 13
Full Perfect Infinitive
to have been + past participle
I'd prefer to have made the tea myself. > I'd prefer the tea to have been made by me.
Bare Infinitive
be +past participle
I'd rather make the tea myself. > I'd rather the tea be made by me.
Bare Perfect infinitive
havebeen +pastparticiple
I'd rather have made the tea myself. > I'd rather the tea have been made by me.
Gerund
being +past participle
I dislike people telling me lies,>I dislike being told lies.
Perfect Gerund
having been + past participle
Iremembersomeonehaving askedmeabout it.>Irememberhavingbeen
asked about it.
ALERT!
perfect continuous. We either use the active voice or we say the same thing in a different way.
The police had been investigating the disappearance of Mr Knight for a number of weeks.
The disappearance of Mr Knight had been under investigation for a number of weeks.
When we use phrasal verbs and other verbs that take dependent prepositions, do not forget to include the
particle or preposition.
The firm took him on.>He was taken on.
Who did they give the job to? > Who was the job given to?
When an active verb has a direct and an indirect object, there are two possible passive forms. This is common
with the following verbs: award, give, lend, offer, pay,promise, refuse, send, show, tell. When the indirect object
becomes the subject of a passive verb, the directobject is usually introduced with a preposition.
They promised Jamie a promotion. (direct object = Jamie; indirect object - a promotion)
Jamie was promiseda promotion.
Apromotion was promised to Jamie.
In spoken English, we sometimes replace the verb be with the verb get in the passive voice.
Did you hear the news? Tim got arrested last night!
. Note that some verbs, such as arrive, come, disappear, go, happen, have, live, occur, sleep, which do not take an
object, cannot be used in the passive.
LET, HELP, MAKE
The verb let (= allow) cannot be used in the passive voice.We must usebe allowed to.
I let my secretary leave early.>My secretary was allowed to leave early.
With the verbs help and make, there is a difference in the structure used in active and passive sentences. Compare:
ACTIVE (active verb + object +bare infinitive)
help
I helped Jane carry the heavy boxes.
make
I make my children tidy their rooms.
PASSIVE (passive verb +full infinitive)
help
Jane was helped to carry the heavyboxes.
make
My children are made to tidy their rooms.
BY VS WITH
We use by after a passive verb form
when we want to say who or what does the action.
The competition was won by Jennifer Hawkins.
The tree was struck by lightning.
when it is important toknowwho completed theaction.
The stadium was financed by the local government.
This beautiful painting was given to us by my grandparents.
when we want to say how something will be achieved.
Our sales can be improved by providing better customer service.
Weusewithaftera passiveverbform
•to say what instrument, tool, piece of equipment, etc
The surface had been damaged with a sharp knife.
was used to do an action.
The shirt wasstained with blue ink.
after pastparticiples such aspacked,filled, crowded,
Thestadiumwaspackedwith supporters.
covered,decorated, etc.
The cakes were covered with chocolate icing.
Theroomwasdecoratedwithfreshflowers.

## Página 218

WordOrder/Inversion/CleftSentences/
Emphasis
WORD ORDER:STATEMENTS
.The word orderin a simple sentenceis usually subject +verb + object.
Marcus enjoys skydiving
The word order in a more complex sentence is usually subject + verb + object + adverb of manner + place + time.
Thecaptain sailed the shipcarefully through the Corinth Canal this morning.
EXAMPLE
We can put time words and adverbs at the
During the voyage the seas were very rough.
beginning or at the end of a sentence.
The seas were very rough during the voyage.
We can put adverbs of frequency before the main
We frequently go rafting along the river.
verb, but after the verb be.
We are frequently at the river, rafting.
Verbs and objects cannot be separated.
I'm launching this product in the summer.
(I'm launching in the summer this product: X)
WORDORDER:QUESTIONS
EXAMPLE
Yes/No questions
auxiliary/modal verb + subject + verb + clause
Does Henry enjoy diving?
Can I help you with anything?
Questions with question words
• Subject questions: question word + verb + clause
Who invited you to come surfing with us?
What happened last night?
Where does Marianna live?
Non-subject questions:question word + auxiliary/modal verb
Why have you worn high heels?
+ subject + verb (+ the rest of the sentence)
When did you buy this beautiful painting?
Indirect questions
Introduced by a phrase or a direct question; use the word order
I wonder how old the little girl is.
of a statement;use if/whether when there is no question word
Can you tell me whether George is married?
May I ask if what time the boat departs?
ALERT!
Question words are not only used in questions. They can be used in clauses that act as the object or the subject of
a verb.
I don't know who took your parking space.
She won't tell me where she's hidden my birthday present!
Why you agreed to this is beyond me.
How you decide to live your life is none of my business.
The suffix -ever and the phrase on earth are often used with question words to demonstrate surprise or irritation.
This does not apply to the question word whose.
Whatever were you thinking?
Why on earth did you buy that dress?

## Página 219

Grammar
INVERSION
There are certain adverbs and adverbial expressions with negative, restrictive or emphatic meaning that are used at the
beginning of sentences for emphasis. When this is the case, the verb comes before the subject. This is called inversion.
Inversion is often used in formal or written English.
EXAMPLE
Rarely/Seldom
Rarely have we seen such a wonderful performance.
Hardly (ever)
Little did I know that the man sitting by me on the tube was the man who
Never
would interviewme that day.
Little
Not only is Kerry a good employee, (but) she is (also) a great mother.
Not only ..., (but ... also)..
At no time
At no time did he indicate that he knew the victim.
In no way
Nowhere will you find better service.
Nowhere
On no account should you stop taking your medicine.
On no account
Under no circumstances must this door be opened,
Under no circumstances
Hardly/Scarcely...when
Hardly had I got through passport control when the flight was called.
No sooner ... than
Scarcely had we left the house when it started to rain.
(used with the past perfect to indicate one
No soonerhad Oliver dropped off to sleep than his phonerang and woke
action happens immediately after another)
him up.
So + adjective/adverb...that...
So hot was it that the students jumped into the lake!
So fast did he drive that we got there in no time.
Such
Such was my embarrassment that I left the room immediately.
(-so much/so great)
Only + time expression
Only later was Clare able to join us.
Only+prepositional phrase
Only after speaking to herfather was John allowed to take Elsa out for dinner.
Only by working steadily did we meet our deadline.
Only if
Only if you aren't scared of heights, should you try bungee jumping.
Not until
Not until we reached the summit, did we realise howfar we'd climbed.
[(inversion in the main clause)
OTHERINSTANCESOFINVERSION
EXAMPLE
after here, there
Here comes your sister.
There goes the bus.
after so,neither,nor
Helen is a risk taker and so is her sister.
(to express agreement)
'Jenna can't come to the party''Nor can I'
afteras
James believes in taking chances, as do l.
(= and the same is true for)
after direct speech
"Where are you going on holiday?' asked George.
'lt's been a worrying time, said Lisa.
in conditional sentences
Were I to have the opportunity, I would grab it. (= If I were to have .../lf I had ...)
(auxiliary or modal verb comes
ShouldyouhearfromSarah,pleasecontactme immediately. (=ifyou (should)hear...)
before subject to replace if)
Had I known you would be late, I'd have gone on my own. (= If I had known ..)
ALERT!
We do not use inversion after here and there when the subject is a pronoun. This is also the case after direct speech.
Where is your father? Oh, here he comes. (Here comes he. X)
"Where is your father?' she asked. ("Where is your father?' asked she. X)
We do not use inversion after as when the two subjects are the same.
Dean is going to Italy again for the summer, as he does every year.

## Página 220

Unit 14
CLEFT SENTENCES
Cleft sentences are used to help us emphasise a particular part of a sentence. They often begin with It, What, All or The.
EXAMPLE
It + be + emphasised word/phrase + whol
George scored the winning goal.
that/which/etc
It was George who scored the winning goal. (= emphasises George)
It was the winning goal that George scored.
(= emphasises which goal George scored)
What clause + be + (to) infinitive/noun
What you should do now is (to) consult a doctor.
(what - the thing that)
What you need is/are good friends.
All + clause + be
All Stacey did was get up and leave the room.
All I want is to live a peaceful life.
The (onlyllast/best) thing + be
The only thing is how are we going to pay for this?
Thebest thing about the hotel was the wonderful service.
What happened + be + clause
He lost control of the car.
(to emphasise a whole sentence)
What happened was (that) he lost control of the car.
ALERT!
. We can also use negative structures in sentences beginning with It and What.
It wasn't me that took the money!
What you mustn't do is jump to conclusions.
FRONTING
Fronting refers to moving part of a sentence to the beginning or front in order to emphasise it. We often front adjective and
adverbial phrases.When this is the case, we invert the subject and the verb be.
EXAMPLE
comparative and superlative
I didn't like her latest novel. Much betterwas her first one.
Sport is great. Best of all is volleyball.
Also + adverbial phrase
Parents and teachers attended theconcert.A/soattheperformancewerethe
mayor and his wife.
adverbial phrases expressingposition
In the corner of the room was an old piano.
participle forms of verbs of movement
Speeding past us on the motorway was a brand new Ferrari.
and position
Nailed to the wall was a sign that said KEEP OUT!
full infinitive clause
To be chosen for the football team was his only ambition.
bare infinitive
'I said I would win'"And win you did!
(when echoing an earlier verb)
ALERT!
We can also front adjectives and adverbs using the word as or though to show contrast and concession.
Sad as she was, she put a smile on her face. (=Although she was sad, sheput a smile on her face.)
Hard though he worked, he could not meet his deadline. (= Although he worked hard, he could not meet his deadline.)
OTHERTYPESOFEMPHASIS
EXAMPLE
Auxiliary verbs can beused in affirmative statements to
Lucy didn't call me back, but she did email me the next day.
emphasise.When speaking,they are stressed.
I do like your outfit! You look great!
Reflexives can be used to emphasise a noun or a pronoun
after the subject
Despite helping to run the tennis club, Jenny herself doesn't
playtennis.
at the end of the clause
I can't keep sorting out your problems. I suggest you find a
solution yourself.
