While we are all aware that our physical (1) characters / characteristics are
passed on to us from our parents, can the same be said for our personality?
The environment in which you grow up has a lasting effect on your (2) attitude /
altitude to the world around you and your (3) conduct / treatment towards others.
One ofthe oldestarguments in the history of psychology is thenature versus
(4) nurture / environment debate. In other words, is our (5) temperature /
temperament already programmed into our DNA or is it primarily influenced by
our life experiences? The coding of our genes determines the different (6) traits /
details that we have -physical (7) insights / attributes like eye colour, height,
nose shape and so on. However, it is still not known whether the more abstract
elements like our (8)depression/disposition,our intelligence and personal
preferences are gene-coded into our DNA, too, or whether they are a result of our
physical,emotional andpsychological environment.Themost likely explanation,
however, is that our (9) behaviour / conditioning is a combination of both factors.

## Página 233

Connectors&Conjunctions
relationshipbetween words,phrases, sentences or clauses and can express things such as contrast, purpose, cause, etc.
.Some linking words, such as and, or, but, nor,for, so and yet, may be used to join words, phrases and clauses that are
grammatically similar in a sentence.
My Fshirts are all black and white.
Will you wear this short-sleeved shirt or that long-sleeved one?
I liveon thetopfloorof a block offlats,yetI hateheights!
.Other linking words and phrases, such as although,because, so, that and even if,join a subordinate clause to the main
clause. When the subordinate clause comes at the beginning of the sentence,it is followed bya comma.
He lives in a small one-bedroomed flat although he can afford a bighouse.
Even though he can afford a big house, he lives in a small one-bedroomed flat.
.Other linking words, such as however, therefore and furthermore, can either modify a whole sentence or connect two
sentences together.They are usually followed by a comma.
She spent many years abroad.However, in later life she returned to her native France.
.Other structures such as both ...and, neither ... nor and not only .. but also are madeup of two parts, but they also link
two parallel structures.
Neither Sally nor her husband liked the people who had moved in next door.
Teresa not only enjoyed holidaying in Tenerife,but shealsoplanned to live there one day.

FORM

• so (that) + clause
Remember to take yourkeys with you so (that) you don't wake

in order (not) tolso as (not) to + bare infinitive

when you get home.
to + bare infinitive

ALERT!
To + bare infinitive cannot be used with not for a negative purpose. We must use in order.not to or so as not to +
bare infinitive.
We'll have something to eat before we go in order not to get hungry later on.
We'll have something to eat before we go so as not to get hungry later on.
(We'll have something to eat before we go not to get hungry later on. X)
EXPRESSINGCONTRAST&CONCESSION
FORM
EXAMPLE
. althoughleven though/though + clause

Even if we find a house both Tom and I like, we can't afford to live in
London.
·despitelin spite of + noun/gerund

commute to work.
In spite of the high cost, we rent a flat in the city centre.
despite thefact that/in spite of the fact that +
In spite of thefact thatI live in the city centre, it still takes me half an
clause
hourto commutetowork.

an artist.
nomatterhow/what/when/where+ clause

whatever/whereverlhowever + clause
Whatever you decide, he'll overrule your decision.
·howeverlnonetheless/nevertheless/still +clause
I'll support your choice of university.Still,Istrongly believe you
should opt for a British one.
however +adjective/adverb + clause
We'llfinish the marathon howeverdifficult it proves tobe!
contrarytolin contrast to + noun
Contrary to what we had believed, Natalie isn't eligible for a scholarship.
on the contrary + clause

I needa newmobilephone.Even so, I'd prefera smart watch formy

bought me a mobile phone.

•adjective/adverb +as/though + clause
Hot though it was, Kim didn't go swimming.
given that + clause
Given that I skipped lunch, I really didn't feel that hungry.
·unlike +noun

## Página 257

Spelling Rules
Present simple
Inthethirdperson singularofthepresentsimple,inaffirmativesentences:
ALERT!
. We add -es to verbs ending in -ch, -ss, -sh, -x and -o.
If theverb ends in a
touch→touches
dress → dresses
wash→washes
vowel + -y, we add -s.
fix →fixes
do does
pay→pays
If a verb ends in a consonant + -y, we take off the -y and add -ies.
buy → buys
hurry→hurries
study → studies
fly→flies
say → says
Verb + -ing
Toformthe-ingformofaverb:
If a verb ends in -e, we take off the -e and add -ing.
drive → driving
ride →riding
smile → smiling
ALERT!
If the verb ends in two
If a verb has one syllable and ends in one vowel + consonant, we double the
vowels + consonant,
final consonant and add -ing.
we do not double the
hit →hitting
win →winning
swim→ swimming
final consonant.
If a verb has two or more syllables and ends in one vowel + consonant,
wait → waiting
wedoublethefinal consonant andadd-ing ifthestress is onthefinal syllable.
wear→wearing
If the stress is not on the final syllable, we do not double the final consonant.
rain → raining
begin →beginning
openopening
If a verb ends in -l, we double the -I and add -ing.
travel → travelling
ALERT!
Verb + -ed
If the verb ends ina
Toform the past simple of regular verbs:
vowel + -y, we add -ed.
stay→ stayed
.We add -d to verbs ending in -e.
enjoy → enjoyed
dance→ danced
hope→hoped
live→lived
play → played
If a verb ends in a consonant + -y, we take off the -y and add -ied.
cry → cried
study→studied
hurry→hurried
ALERT!
Ifaverbhasonesyllableandendsinonevowel+consonant,wedoublethe
If a verb ends in -w or
final consonant and add -ed.
-x, we do not double
plan →planned
stop→ stopped
rob→robbed
the final consonant.
If a verb ends in -l, we double the -I and add -ed.
snow→ snowed
travel→travelled
fix→fixed
mix →mixed
Comparison of adjectives
Toformthe comparativeand superlativeform of adjectives:
If an adjective ends in -e, we add-r for the comparative form and -st for the superlative form.
nice→nicer→nicest
large→larger→largest
If an adjective has one syllable and ends in one vowel + consonant, we double thefinal consonant and add-er
or -est.
hot→hotter→hottest
big → bigger →biggest
If an adjective ends in -y, we take off the -y and add -er or -est.
happy→happier→happiest
heavy→heavier→heaviest
Adverbsofmanner
.If an adjective ends in -y, we take off the-y and add -ily to form the adverb ofmanner.
noisy → noisily
happy→happily
Ifanadjectiveendsin-l,weadd-ly.
wonderful →wonderfully (wonderfulyx)
beautiful →beautifully (beautifuly X)
